CONTRACT | 


BRIDGE 


By W. E. McKenney. 


Secretary, American Bridge . 
agus. 


Pre-cmptive bids of four of 4 


sult atomade primary te shut ont. yesterday’s Rotary Club luncheon, 


the opponents. They. shauld 
made only on hands containing 4 
long suit and little or no defen- 
sive strength. ; 

They indicate to partner that 
with the sult you name fa trump 
you will take eight tricks—in other 
words, you are over-bidding your 
hand two tricks, If partner happens 
to Hold two tricks, you may be suc- 
cessful in making your contract. 

Under the writer’s system, it is 
impossible to make a pre-emptive 
bid of three and they rarely, if ever, 
accompHsh their purpose. If you 
are going. to pre-empt, do so high 
enough to keep the opponents from 
arriving at their best declaration. 

There are a few hands where pre- 
emptive bids of four in a minor 
will work out well, but generally 
the best minor pre-emptive bid is 
un original bid of five. You are 
only endeavouring, to accomplish 


one thing with a pre-emptive bid,- 


and that is to stop the opponents 
from bidding. 


Support Pre-Emptive Bids 


As partner has told you that he 
ia overbidding his hand two tricks 
and has no side strength, do not 
support his pre-emptive bid 
though you hold quick tricks, un- 
lesa you hold a sufficient number of 
quick tricks to assure a slam, The 
main point to remember in pre- 
emptive bids ix that if the op- 
ponents should overcall partner's 
original suit bid of four and your 
hand contains only one to two 
quick tricks, you can make a good 
sacrifice bid by supporting partner. 
With partner advertising no defen- 
sive atrength and you with very 
little, undoubtedly the opponents 
have game. 

When you support partner's bid, 
holding one trick, you can expect 
to go down only two tricks, and in 
the majority of cases for a very 
good sacrifice. If you-hold «two 
tricka, partner should be down only 
one, 

Pre-emptive bids can also be 
made after the bidding is opened; 
e. g. partner bids one heart, oppo- 
ments pass, and you bid three,or 
four spades. In either case, this 


would be purely a pre-emptivo bid. 


informing partner that your hand 
was worthless for anything but 
spades, There are very few hands, 
however, where this will prove suc- 
ceasful as ‘generally the only one 
it shuts out from bidding is. part- 
ner. : ; 

Again, supposing that opponents 
opened with one heart. If you bid 
three or four spades, this would be 
two or three more than necessary, 
and would in no way be a forcing 
bid. Pariner would immediately 
it for a pre-emptive bid in 
which: you were-announeing - that 
you had nothing but spades. This 
type of bid is used more frequently 
than the overcall of partner’s suit 
with a pre-empt.

PLAYGROUNDS FOR | 


COLONY 


ROTARY. CLUB DECISION 
NOT UNANIMOUS. 


H.K. PRE-HISTORY . 


The question of providing more 
children’s playgrounda in the 
Colony came up for discussion at 


which was held in Messrs. Lane 
Crawford’s restaurant under the 
chairmanship of Sir William Hor- 
nell. It was unanimously decided 
to maintain the two existing 
grounds, but a resolution for pro- 
viding two more met with! some 
dissent. ; Sos 

The Chairman welcomed the 
following visitors—-The Rev. E. C. 
H. Tribbeck, Capt. Hopkins, of the 
#8. Yatshing, Rotarian G. J, Mec- 
Carthy and Rotarian Yinson Lee, 
both of Shanghai: : 

The Chairman said: On August 
26, the Club carricd the following 
resolution unanimously: “That ap- 
plication be made “to the Govern- 
ment for the use of Blake Gardens 
and an areca opposite the Soldiers’ 
and Snilors’: Home on the Praya 
East Reclamation; that the Govern- 
ment be asked to enclose and level 
these grounds, and that the Rotary 
Club equip them with games ap- 
Paratus and pay the salary of a 
Play Director by a levy -on each 
member of the Club, and that the 
Chinese Y.M.C.A. be Invited ‘to 
secure the Director and supervise 
his. work.” : 

You will notice that the Rotary 
Club’s undertaking to equip the 
playgrounds with games apparatus 
and pay the salary of a Play Direc- 
tor was not limited to any speeffic 
period. However, on writing pri- 
vately, as I did, on August 26 Inst, 
to the Hon. Colonial Secretary, I 
said that the Rotary Club's 
guarantee to find the cost of the 
initial apparatis and the salaries 
of a Play Director and grounds- 
men was for a year. ‘1 added I had 
no doubt that if the experiment was 
successful, and I was sure. it would 
be successful; that the Rotary 
Club would assume practically 
permanent responsibility for this 
work, which it would almost cer- 
tainly try to extend. 


Extension Recommended. 


You have before you, in The 
Bulletin, certain 8 of the 
Community _ Serifiée.- mittee. 
The first recommendation is that 
the present work be maintained. 
The third recomnierdation Is that 
the present arrangements with the 
Chinese Y-.M.C.A. be continued. 
The second recommendation {s that 
{wo new grounds be applied for, and 
fourthly that the amount required 
be raised by voluntary subscriptions 
from members of this Club. 

Now, I hold that the Club has 
committed itself to running, as 
organised playgrounds, the exist- 
ing ground at Wanchai and that 
which occupies half of Blake Gar- 
dens. I propose, however, to put 
it to the Club— whether. or -not the 
existing playground work should 
be maintained and maintained on 
the same basis, that’ ia to say at a

JAPAN MOVING IN

_ Shanghal, May 10. 
Apprehonsion that Shanhal- 
kunan might, at any moment, be 
; como tho centre of another Sino 
'Japanoso “incident,” has been 
,ereated by reports alleging that 
\Japaneso military _authoritics 
{have demanded the right to sup- 
press and arrest Chinese 'Volun- 
teers” within an area ‘of 27 miles 
west of the Great Wall, 
Gonerol Ho Chu-kuo, Chincac 
Commander-in-Chief of tho 
Shanhaikuan ‘aren, has rofuacd 
; to comply, and now ho fs awnit- 
‘ing the Japancso military -au- 
‘thorities’ next step. Si 
It is reported that the Japancsc 
forces are strengthening their 
defences in the vicinity . of 
Shanhaikuan and that under 
their protection, 100 Manchukuo 
police have occupied the Shan- 
haikuan railway station. 
Tho situation is described 
tense.—Renter’s Special. 


Péhing Reports. 


Peking, May 10. 

‘Vernacular newspapers atate 
that a “certain high official” went 
to Nanking by air a few daya ago, 
and, ft is suggested -in some cin 
cles, he intends to discusa the 
Shanhaikuan situation with the 
Central ‘Government. 

dapanese military authoritfos 
are continually accusing the Chi-, 
nese authoritier at}  Shanhal- 
| kuan, of allowing large numbers 
of volunteers and munitions to 
enter Manchuria, and are report. 
ed to he threatening drastic 
action —Rexter. , 


War Office Explains, 


Tokyo, May 10. 
. Announcing the arrival of tho 
j main body of the Fourteenth 
Division at Dairen from Shang- 
hai, the War Office explains that 
the transfer, of reinforcements to 
Manvhuria was necessitated by 
the inadequacy of the Manchukuo 
police military to cope with the 
increasing activities of bandits 
and insurgents who, with the 
growth of the spring foliage, are 
able to conceal’ their movements 
and carry out their depredations: 
over a greatly extended area. 
The War Office states’ that 
there are now six divisions in 
Manchuria and: Korea.—Reuter,

Leader's Statement. , 


Shanghni, May 10, 

A Korean revolutionary leader 
ia reported to have issued a state- 
ment, admitting that the recent 
bombing affair which occurred at 
Hongkew Park was inspired by the 
Korean revolutionary Jeaders. 

In the statement, which was cir- 
cularised to the Chinese. ond 
English newspapers for publica- 
tion, the Korean leader also con- 
feased his participation in and 
inspiration of other attempts on 
the lives of leading stateamen In 
Japan, Including an attempt on 
the life of a former Premier, 

The atatement explains that the 
Korean, was of poor parentage, 
who was given two bombs in the 
morning, one to kill the Japanese 
and the other with which to com- 
mit suicide.—Rensha, :

; Shanghai, May 10. 

A circular telegram issued by 25 
Cantonese leaders, headed by Mr. 
Sino Fu-chen, was received to-day, 
strongly criticising Nanking for 
signing the Shanghai armistice 
agreement. It expresses grent 
dissatisfaction over the fact that 
the Shanghai affair was not sot- 
tled together with the Manchurian 
Imbroglic, and that uo tlme Hmit

SILVER-PROUD. 


Just a few moments given to the 
cate of table silver and cutlery 
every day will halve tho time spent 
on the weekly cleaning. : 

Never put the silver and knives 
away carelessly, or they will scrat- 
ch each other. There is an ex- 
cellent silver tily which is simply 
n board with a row of pegs. Be- 
tween cach is a gap into which 
spoons and forks are fitted, the 
whole leing slipped into a drawer 
in sideboard or dresser, and used 
aga tray when laying the tidle. 

Knivey should be kept in a rack 
which is fitted with slots for each 
blade, This can be put in the cut- 
lery drawer, and na it holds two 
dozen knives it is a quick way of 
keeping a check on the numbers.

WEDNESDAY, MAY 11, 1932. 


THE WATER SITUATION 


Although there is no occasion 
for alarm, the Colony’s water 
supply has su decreased as a con- 
sequence.of the lack of. rain that 
the authoritiea have deemed it 
wise to impose fresh restrictions 
as from to-day... It has been found 
that the recent cutting down of 
the hours of supply, so far from 
resulting in leas water being con- 
sumed, has had a precisely oppo- 
site effect. This is due to hoard- 
ing—the drawing off of supplies 
for use when none is’ coming 
through the mains, much of which 
is Inter thrown away when the 
service is resumed. Past experi- 
ence has shown that this is the 
kind of thing ‘which might be 
expected, howover regrettable it 
may be. The fact is that people 
have not yct been brought to a 
consclousness of the. disservice 
which they do to themselves and 
the community at large by. necd- 
lens wastage of wator. It fs 
enough for most people that water 
periodically: comes through the 
mains, and if it so happena that 
they have put some nalde, then 
thoy make no. bones about dis. 
carding the surplua when fresh 
supplies como through. 

How this problom ia to bo coun- 
tered, it in dificult to sce. More 
ovor, there is no guarantee that 
the same practice’ will not bo 
‘followed under the new reatric- 
tions now being Imposed. It would 
be {mpracticablo to make a byc- 
Inw prohibiting hoarding of 


water, aince the drawing off of a <p remeecanancec mE SNNCRRSNNNC ADT 


certain amount fs necessary for . : , 
i DAY BY DAY 


wiso available. Thero can be no 
possible objection to this prac- 
tiee, provided no more than Is 
actually ‘needed is put aside. -The 
harm is dono when the residue Is 
thrown away. This is shcor 
criminal wastage, but the dificulty 
is to catch the ‘people who 50. 
thoughtlessly impinge on the 
Colony’s supplics. Tho difference 
botweon tho new and the old res- 
trictions: is that in place of o 
fourteen hours’ supply, the com- 
munity will now have to be con- 
tent with seven hours. Provious- 
ly, water was cut off for ten hours 
at_a atretch during tho night; now 
there will be four hours’ supply 
in the morning and three hours 
in the latter part of the day. It 
remains to be seen whether the 
outcome will be any reduction ‘in 
consumption, secing thant most

By CLAUD MULLINS. 


(London Police Court Magistrate 
and Author of “In Quest of 
, Justice’) 


In St. Matthew's Gospel the ad- 
vice is given: “Agrea with thine 
adversary quickly whiles thou art 
in the way with him, leat at any 
time the adversary deliver thee to 
the Judge.” Unfortunately, it {s 
frequently the case that it is im- 
possible to follow this ideal. Tho 
ndversary may not be ‘willing to 
make a compromise, and in this 
world of reality the fact has also 
to be reckoned with that, even when 


intorvention of the Judgo some- 
times becomes necessary. 
The first difficulty needs no com- 
ment. In on imperfect world the 
sottlement of all disputes by mutual 
adjustment is obviously impossible, 
and this by no means necessarily 
implies unreasonableness, us

take a frequent illustration from

Sterling’ Depreciation, 

Since the abandonment of the 
gold standard by the United King- 
dom and the adoption of similar 
muasures elsewhere, notably in the 
Senndinavian countries, some of 
the Latin-American republics and 
Japan, many people have expected 
a general improvement in the 
trade intercourse of. the countries 
concerned. These expectations 
were baacd on the conditions which 
developed .in Germany at the time 
of the immense post-war period 
of inflation. Observers, however, 
tend to overlook one vital differ- 
ence between the position then and 
now. When Germany found her- 
self in the throes of inflation, 
there was a universal demand for 
commodities, due’ to, the partial 
stoppage of world trade during 
the war. At present the world 
finds itself faced with an enor- 
mous over-supply of commodities; 
and the dianatrous economic deve- 
lopment can no longer be put right 
by such palliative (as 4 lowering 
of sterling exchange. British for- 
cign-trade statistics do not indi- 
enate that currency depreciation 
has brought about 2 material in- 
erense in the export trade. The 
statistics . published about the 
middle of January show that, in 
almost all branches of the British 
export trade, an increngs can In- 
deod be recorded whea the last 
quarter of 1931 is compared with 
the immediately preceding quarter, 
but that this increase is—gencral- 
Jy speaking-—but slight and fa due 
to seasonal causes which always 
operate. during the part of the 
year here concerned. On the other 
hand, a comparison with the last 
quarter of 1930 shows that there 
haa been an execedingly large 
decrease in British’ exports, not 
only in regard to conl, pig iron and 
raw, steel, but also in regard to 
machinery. Despite the currency 
depreciation, the experts of agri- 
cultural machinery, motors and 
atcam bollors are oven leas then 
they wore during the third quarter 
of 1931. Similar romarks apply to 
motor-cycles. ‘and locomotives.

pald by both Houses of Parllament 
to-day, In the House of Lorda, an 
address requesting the King to ex- 
press on behalf of the House, 
adhorrence at the crime and 
aympathy with the Government of 
people of France in their grief, 
was moved by Lord Hatisham. He 

“anid the whole world was shocked 
at the news of the murderous at- 
tack on President Doumer and its 
fatal outcome, 

That a noble life given to public 

* service should be atruck down by 
the irresponsible hand of an. as- 
sassin was a shock to the moral 
acnse of civilised communities. 
The late President had Jeft be- 
hind him an memory of personal 

. integrity, patriotism and unflinch- 
ing cournge which could only 
deepen for the French — people 
their sense of loss, but which 
surely would be an inspiration to 
hia country in the days to come. 

Lorda Ponsonby and Reading 
supported the motion.

similar address was moved by’ Mr. 
Stariley Baldwin and seconded by

bury.

-BURTON HOLMES 
‘HERE 


TO MAKE HONGKONG 
TRAVELOGUE 


Burton Holmes, ,the eminent 
traveller, lecturer and motion pic- 
ture photograjher, who recently 
celebrated his forticth senson on 
the lecture platform, arrived in 
Hongkong: on the Empress of Asin 
to-day. : 

As ‘he wanders around tho 
world, to lands and far porta on 
the seven sens, he heads a merry 
parade, for wherever Burton 
Holmes goes, there follows in his 
wake a motley throng, who view 
his travelogue films in the picture 
theatres of the world. 

Burton Holme’s travelogues are 
famous. For seven years, during | 
the sllent cra, he was associated , 
with Paramount, and his weekly 
travel pictures were shown 
wherever Paramount films. were 
screenell. He makes talking 
travel films for the M.G.M. people 
nowadays, and very fine filma they 
are, too, 1s ean be told by those 
who saw his recent film of the 
Yangtse Basin, screened in Hong- 
kong a few weeks ago, 

Mr. Holmes has travelled ex- 
tenaively, and has visited Hong- 
kong on six previous occasions, 
Every now and then he feels the 
urge to “go places,” so now he is 
undertaking, joyously, anothce and 
more comprehensive tour around 
the world, 

This morning in an interview 
with oa representative of the 
Telegraph he recalled that his first 
visit here occurred just after 
Admiral Dewey defeated the 
Spaniards In the  Amerienn- 
Spanish war of 1898. On that 
occasion Mr. Holmes met and 
photographed the famous Amer: . 
can Admiral in Hongkong. 

“I've photographed: Hongkong 
from several angles," he said, 
“and ‘it has always proved one of 
the principal features in my films 
showing how the rest of the world 
lives and what it does. This time 
I'm: going to catch a lot of 1982 
Hongkony, and will incorporate, 
it in a talking travelogue of my: 
round-the-world four, Most of 
my previous films have been short 
travelogues, but this will be a 
longer effort. : 

“Seven langunges are-used in 
sound recording.» [ spenk English, 
French, Spanish and Italian my- 
self, and expert linguists trans- 
late into Portuguese, Swedish 
and German, 

“As oo matter of fact I can 
apenk German also, but we don’t 
get enough beer in America to gel 
the accent right!” 

Mr. Holmes, who is accompanied 
by Andre Ia-Varre, the eminent 
camera-man, who is his partner, 
will proceed from Hongkong to 
the Dutch Kast Indies, via Manila, 
From there he will return home 
‘fa Ceylon, the Suez, Egypt, 
Palestine and the Continent.

{To the Editor, Hongkong

Sir,—With the arrival of ithe 
hot weather, the inconvenience of 
‘carrying about heavy silver dollars 
becomes more pronounced. I 
don’t know whether others feol 
about it as much as I do, but ft fg 
very annoying when changing a 
five-dollar note to have to carry 
about three or four of these big 
silver coins in one’s pockets. 

Visitors’ often complain about 
this, saying that nowhere else -do 
they have to put up with such an 
inconvenience. 

Could not the Banks relieve us 
from this burden? There may be 
a restriction on the note Issue, 
but, if not, could not the Banks. be 
induced to put more one-dollar 
notes in circulation? I feel sure 
that. everybody would feel thank- 
ful for such relief.—Yours, etc, 

Hor Anp Tinep.

deed, its main function seems like.

fault already. 

Austrian may be expected to go

ternal debt payments due in the 
next siX months, 

Greece is not being encouraged

MR. SMITH'S WAY 

TO PAY DEBTS 
“THINLY DISGUISED 
* CANCELLATION.” 


Washington, Apr. 14. 
Mr. Alfred Smith’s proposal that 


America should forget about Euro- | - 


pean debts for 20 years, and 
should write off sums equal to 25 
per cent. of the value of American 
xoods purchased by Europe, has not 
received wide attention here. 

Opponents of the schemo describo 
it as thinly disguised cancellation 
and consider that the approval 
given It by the Wall Street interests 
in.the party is sufficient condemna- 
tion. 

Senators who comment privately 
an the proposals dismiss tham as 
impracticable. = 

A. specch by Senator Hiram 
Johnson to-day reflected the opinion 
of the majority of the Senate ‘on 
debts, Ri 

“We could have collected over 
$260,000,000 (£60,000,000 at par) 
last yenr if wo had desired,” said 
Senator Johnson; “we can collect 
our $270,000,000 in the middlo of 
this year if we desire to insist upon 
payment.” 

The debate was on the question 
of economy, and Senator’ Johnson 
was arguing that economies which 
would Iead to American Govern- 
ment employees being thrown out 
of work could have been avoided if 
the moratorium had not been 
granted. A twenty year mora- 
torium was tantamount to eancella- 
tion, Senator Johnson anid. He 
was opposed even to na year’s ex- 
tension, which he predicted would 
be suggested by the Administra- 
tion. : 

Democrats at War. 

‘As regards Mr. Smith's speech 
last night, more interest is taken 
here in the domestic implications. 
The Press of the country | unnni- 
mousaly interpret it as a direct ‘at- 
tack on Governor Roosevelt of New 
York, who is at present the most 
likely Democratic — candidate * fer 
the Presidency. War between, the 
two wings of the Democratic Party 
has now been brought Into the 
open. ad 

“L have recently stated,” said Mr.- 
Smith, “that while I would accept 
nomination if tendered by the Con- 
vention, that until the Convention 
axsembled I would not be for or 
agyninst any candidate. I announce 
to-night all exception to that. state- 
ment. ' I will take off my coat and 
walstcoat and fight to the. end 
against any candidate who persists 
in any demagogic appeal to ‘the 
masees of the working people of 
this country’ to'destroy themselves 
iby setting classy against class, rich 
against poor. 

While in the early part of his 
speech Mr. Smith allowed himself 
freé play of voice and gesture, he 
read the above statement carefully 


. from a typed copy, evidently feel- 


ing that it was his ‘most important. 
contributias 

The Roosevelt faction, who rely 
for support on the Dry South and 
the Radical Weat, profess {o belleve 
that the speech will do more good 
to Governor, Roosevelt than harm, 
but supporters. of Mr. Smith 
‘rejoice to find him back once 
more in fighting form, while busi- 
ness, which fears that under Gov- 
-ernor Roosevelt the Democratic 
Party would become ‘a museum of 
diseredited economic and political 
theories, heaves a sigh of relief.

WALDECK ROUSSEAU | 
IN HONGKONG 


The French , armoured cruiser 
Waldeck Rousseau has arrived hore 
from Shanghai. The fingahip of 
Vice-Admira) Herr, Commandor-in- 
Chief of the French Naval Forces 
in Far Eastern waters, sho {gp pro- 
ceeding back to France, via Saigon, 
on being decommissioned, the Vico- 
‘Admiral being rolleved by Admiral 
Commander-in- 
Chief, who is expected here towards 
the end of the month on the cruiser 
Primauguct. 

‘This is the Inst occasion on which 
Hongkong will bo seeing the 
Waldeck Rousreau, which, with hor 
six funnela, has stood out con- 
spleuously from amongst the many 
mival visitors to_ this port. In 
point of fact, the Waldeck Rousseau 
in a very old type of vessel, one of 
the heavily armoured type, which in 
pre-wnr days took a middle place 
between, the dreadnought and 
ordinary crufger. | 

Built in T90K, 

Built in 1908, she has fourteén 
7.G-Inch guna, eight 3-inch guns and 
two «anti-aircraft -guns. Her 
secondary armament consists of 
10 9-pounders, 2 8-pounders and 
1 l-pounder. She 1s heavily 
armoured, a 634-inch bolt protect- 
ing her vitals, while the .turrets, 
turret bases and casemates are Cor- 
respondingly well protected, — the 
conning tower velng specially rein- 
forced by a ten-inch plate. 

The cruiser displaces alightty 
over 12,000 tons, as compared with 
the 10,000 ton elass of cruiser nae 
thorised by the Washington Treaty 
and of which there ave at present 
five included in the British 
rquindron on the Chinn Station, 

The Waldeck Rousseau wis to 
have been scrapped some fow yours 
ago, but for reasons of economy the 
Franch Minister of Marine decided 
to put her again into commission 
for n new lease of fe on the Far 
Enstern Service. She was recon- 
ditfoned and a few improvements 
ndded such as the instalment of 
two anti-aircraft guns and the ad- 
dition of two seaplanes to her, 
equipment, Since her arrival here 
she hns‘served as the fingship of 
two Admirals, Viee-Admiral Herr 
being the last to fly his ‘pennant 
from aboard her. It is announced 
that her career will terminate on 
her return to France, when sho will 
be broken up.

Canton, May 10. 

- Troop movements on the West

Chal-tong have been dispatched to

monta of othcr gunboats under 
Admiral) Chan Chak, whose re-

the Government post of high ad- 
viger to the new naval department 
havo raised tho possibility of 
union between the navy and Gen- 
eral Chang Wol-cheung, for reais- 
tance toe the Government measures 
for tho reorganisation of their 
‘dopartments. 

Kwangsi Lendern’ Role. 


Many believe that the next de- 
velopment in tho South-western 
aituation may come from Kwangei 
and in this connexion General 
Chan Chai-tong has sent Mr. Feng 
Chu-wan, the recently resigned 
Finance Commisaloner, to Nan- 
ning to interview Generals Li 
Chung-yen and Pei Chung-hsl. 
The appointment of Mr. Fong 
Chu-wan to an important military 
post here has been hinted at in 
semt-official circles .and thin has 
been regarded as an effort to re- 
tain thé friendly bond with ‘the 
Kwangsi Military Party, of which 
Mr. Feng Chu-wan has been for 
a number of years a staunch stip- 
porter.

NOTES SUPPLIED BY THE

That “outside noiaca” are not an un- 
mitigated evil to movie companies on 
location ‘was illustrated: during the 
filming of.n scena of “Over The fn 
which opens next Sunday at tho 
King's Theatro, with Jamés Dunn, 
Sally Eilers and Moo Marsh in the 
leading rolew . 

Tho scene, taken in the little moun- 
tain villago of Sonora, Valifornia, u 
very solemn one, called for Mac Marrh 
and James Dunn to descent! the steps 
of a country courthouse, : 

When Director Henry King shouted, 
“eamora," everything seemed to 
perfect. No one could think of an 
addition that would further the scone’s 
solemnity. It remained? for accident 
to make that addition. As the heart- 
broken mother nnd son started down 
tho steps the deop-toned boll of tha 
courthouse, which dates ‘way back to 
gold-rush days, chimed the hour. And 
the twelfth stroke of noon sounded 
just as the Prison van's door closed 
behind Dunn‘s back.

ALARMING EXPERIENCE OF 
AIR LINER 


’ London, May 10. 

Further details regarding the 
Impcrial -Alrways 38-seater air 
liner Horatius, which was struck 
by lightning yesterday when 2,000 
fect up over Kent on ita way to 
Paris, showed that the pilot, 
Captain Jonos, one of the Airway’s 
most experienced airmen, was un- 
perturbed by the unusual ox- 
perience. Tho visibility was nil, 
and tho machine was proceeding 


be| through heavy slect storm, when a 


blinding flash oceurred which dis- 
abled the radio equipment, com- 
pletely blow out: the cockpit win- 
dows and slightly damaged two of 
the propellers, 

The pilot: scribbled no note 
telling tho passongers that evdry- 
thing was all right, and that ho 
waa flying to Croydon. 

This ho did, despite thick 
weather and a uscltss compass, 
making a perfect landing. Thir- 
teen passengers including several 
women had remained quite calm, 
and at once transferred to another 
air-liner In which Captain Jones 
flew them to Paris.” No one was 
hurt and the machine was damaged 
but repairable —British Wireless.

taken for granted. Treated, for-

jist the way that I happen’ to

“The Night Angel.” 


The King’s Thentre offers for the 
public delectation one of the most ab- 
norbing and exciting talkles of the 
current season—"The Night Angel,” 
with Nancy Carroll and Fredric March 
in the leading starring roles. : 

In addition to the fact that the 
piquant Miss Carroll and the hand- 
some Mr. March are in the. picture 
thero is the interest which accrues to 
the name of Edmund Goulding, the 
directorial genius who wrote and di- 
rected this romantic drama of modern 
days and nights in Prague, the modern 
capital of Czecholslovakia. 

Goulding was the author and direc- 
tor of Gloria Swanson’s well-remem- 
bered play “The Trespasser,” and af 
the Nancy Carroll-Phillips Holmes 
record-break—~"The Devil's Holidayd 

In “The Night Angel” Goulding has 
chosen an entirely new locale, a fresh 
Viewpoint in talkie stories. 

“The Night Angel” is Nancy's third 
picture to be made in Parniount's 
New York studio, The others wero 
“Laughter” with March, and “Stolen 
Heaven” with Phillips Holmes, 

March was recently made a star by 
Paramount as the result of his re- 
markably splendid work’ in “The 
Royal Family of Broadway,” “Man- 
slaughter,” “Laughter,” “Honour 
Among Lovers,” and others. : 

A competent cast of well-known 
Broadway performers supports the 
stars. : 

“The Painted Desert” 


Bill Boyd returned to his old stamp- 
Ing ground when he went to the desert 
outside of Flagataff, Arizona, for 
location scenes of “The Painted De- 
sert,” hix new Pathe star -picture 
which will. be on view at the Ceritral 
Theatre to-morrow. 

Before Boyd took up motion pictures 
as a carcer be was more or less of a 
soldier of fortune and for several 

ears tried his luck in and aroun 
“lagstatY where he swung a double 
edged axe in the lumber camps. 

After considerable experience as a 
woodchopper Bill acquired a dunce hall 
at Lake Mar, n resort cight miles 
from Flagstaff. This wax one of the 
first places he visited when he reached 
the Arizona town. It was here that 
the star held a powwow reunion with 
several dozen of hia cronies of the 
days when a chuck wagon looked like 
the Ritz to him. . 

Helen Twelvetrees plays opposite 
Boyd in “The Painted Desert.’ Others 
in the cast are William Farnum, J. 
Farrell Macdonald, Clarke Gable, Will 
Walling, Charles Sellon, James Don- 
Inn; Wade Boteler; Richard Cramer, 
George Burton, « William “LeMaire, 
Breese, Al St, John and others. .

should be done. It’s not the cave-

eynics. They haven't the faintest

to lift one finger and o great big

TREASURY BONDS. 


ENCOURAGING RESPONSE TO 
SALE BY TENDER 


London, May 10. 

The response to the three. per 
cent. Treasury Bonds, offered for 
sale by tender last week, proved 
Much greater than was expected. 
Applications amounted to £120,- 
140,000 and tenders were accept- 
ed to the extent of £110,000,000, 


ad{Which realised an average price 


of £97 16s. 1.970, per cent. or 18. 
1,974. above the minimum price. 
The amount subscribed fo 
largely exceeds the estimates 
formed by the moncy market that 
it was, suggested the Government 
departments might have taken a 
major portion of the bond. This 
sugyestion has been disposed of 
by the Secretary to the Treasury, 
who, in‘a reply to a House of 
Commons question stated that the 
tote] amount subscribed by the 
Government department, or in- 
stitutions acting under the  re- 
commendations or instructions, of 
the Government, was lesa than 
£10,000,000.—Reuter.

Hongkong’s Pre-History. 


Owing to the Ma,or portion of 
the time having been taken up with 
the playground discussion, an nul. 
dress entitled “The Pre-History of 
Hongkong,” by Professor J, 1. 
Shellshear, was unavoidably cur- 
tailed, and the speaker was not able 
to make more than brief references 
to his subject. 

He explained that the subject 
dealt’ with the. cultures of early 
man, and explained that 20 polished 
stone axe heads had been found in 
Hongkong. In a period of about 
five years something like 4,000 
specimens had been. found in the 
Colony. . He referred to interesting 
finds at Castle Peak of stone im- 
plements of small diameter which, 
he said, were almost exactly similar 
to the oldest cultural implements 
known in Indo-China, 

Professor Shellshear ulso stated 
that x bronze burial ground had 
been found at Aberdeen,, where 
bronze axes, spears and swords had 
been found. : 


".. philippine Islands.

spectmens had been found in. the

that interest in the subject lay in

SUN’S EFFECT ON 
"WIRELESS | 


DIFFICULT TIME AHEAD 


Wireless engincers aro becoming 
nlarmed at happenings oF tho sun. 
The coming period of minfmum 
aunspot activit: 

in tho 
ay 
future of long-distance tranamis- 
sion on ehort waves. ° 

‘Within tho next two years tho 
“question will arise whother large 
tommoreial concerns. W li not have 
to revise drastically their catimates 
-for the periodic reconstruction of 
atations to fit in with the sun’s 
cycle or even abandon short waves 
as a permanent basis of long-dis- 
tance communication. 

Sun-spots have attained to com- 
mercial importance becauso they 
affect ‘the sun’s radiation which in 
turn affects the electrically-charge 
lnyera of the earth's atmosphere. 
These layers reficct wireless waves 
and determine’ the distance fram 
tho transmitting station at which 
they return to the surface of the 
earth, 

‘As a result of this eloven-year 
cycle the years from 1924 to 1928 
naw a steady improvement In long- 
distance short-wave conditions and 
a corresponding reduction in the 
wave-lengths which could be com- 
mercially used, for example, on tho 
North Atlantic route to the nelgh- 
bourhood of 18 metres. | 


A Cycle of Change. 


“Within recont months the con- 
clusion has gained ground,” a pro- 
minent wireless engincer explained 
“that these dovelopmenta have been 
to n large extent illusory. We have 
been forced to realise that the 
gradual rise in practicable wave- 
lengths which hos been taking place 
since 1928 {e in all probability con- 
nected with a definite cycle. 

“The importance of thin dis- 
covery from’ the point of view of 
the necessary re-equipment of the 
big commercial stations within the 
eleven-year cycle needs no empha- 
sis, and it remains to be seen 
whether at the worst period it may 
not be necessary to return to the 
old iden of longer wavelengths and, 
where possible, more power. 

“phe whole trouble is that rals- 
ing the wave-length ‘beyond a cer- 
tain point loses all the advantages 
of short-wave transmission—the 
using low power 
and immunity from interference 
from the electrical 
clouds. : ; 
"The most hopeful fenture in.the 
Bituation is that, the peak of good 
transmission appears to be mu 
leas clearly defined than the peak of 
bad transmission, an example 0 
which {s approaching. This should 
mean that the worst period will be 
over relatively soon. 

“Another possibility is that deve- 
Jopments in polarised waves—which 
vibrate only in one direclion—may 
provide a solution of the problem, 
but this is not yet in sight. - 

“Whatever the effect on practical 
wireless, it appears probable that 
wireless experience Will provide jm- 
portant information

MR. CHAMBERLAIN’S

RAID ON SOCIAL

cial services 4ppeared to be .- 
in the mind of Mr. Neville

fresh economics in Govern- 
ment ‘expenditure, to be

MR: CHURCHILL'S WARNINGS — 


London, May 10, 


During the second duy's debate 
in the House of Commons on the 
second reading of the Finance 
Hil, Mr. Winston Charchill,  for- 
mer Chancellor of the Exchequer, 
made strong demand for inter- 
national action on money problems, ' 

Cupitalist civilisation, he said, 
was adrift and no single Power 
in the world was strong cnough 
to take the helm. 

The strength of three or four 
of the greatest powers would be re- 
quired. 

He did not velieve that in- 
dividual, unrelated inflation or 
individual‘ unrelated credit ex. 
pansion by various nations would 
sglye the problem and the diffienl- 
ties. 

FALL OF PRICES. r 

International uction to arrest 
the fall of prices was the sole 
hope of averting a world crash and 
he urged the Chancellor of Ex- 
chequer to reconsider the decision 
announced during last night's 
debate that at-present a Currency 
Conference could not be called. 

He approved the specific pro- 
posala made by the Government 
and regarded tariffs as necessary, 
but only world action could cure 
the world evil and no such action 
could taken except through the! 
co-operation of  Britnin and the 
United States. 


’ MAJOR ELLIOTT'S HINT. 


‘A further hint that the Govern- 
ment is aiming at international 
monetary co-operation was given 
by the Financial Secretary to the 
Treasury, Major LElliott,. when 
winding up the debate. 

He alluded to Mr. Winston 
Churchill’s, suggestion of co-opera- 
tion with the United States and} 


added that it was the present aim} 
of the. National Government ad-! 
ministration to encourage any at-} 
tempt in the way of friendly col-! 
laboration with friendly nations, 
inside and-outside the Emptre. 


BRITAIN'S STRENGTH. 


Major Elliott drew attention to 
the strength of British yilt-cdged 
securities as contrasted with — the 
position of the premier. sccurities 
of France and the United States. 

He concluded: 


. “There is a peril around us, 
the peril even of the crash of 
Western civilisation is by no 
means yet avoided and dan- 
gers such as we have not yet 
known still lio before us. 


“We are advancing experi- 
mentally. Let us advance 
together.” .

PURCHASING POWERS. 


London, May 7. 


The eminent Swedish cnonomist, 
Professor Gustay.Cassel, in a lec- 
ture ut Oxford to-day on the crisis 
in the world’s monetary system, 
snid that now-a-days the general 
view was that it was an advantage 
to abandon the gold standard in 
order to return to it at. a new 
parity below the Internal purchas- 
Ing power of currency, 

Countries off the gold standard, 
he declared, were eager to suppress 
the external value of their curren- 
cles below the Internal value.

CONTRACT 
BRIDGE 


By. W. E. McKenney. 


— 


Secretary, American Bridge 
League. 


Preemptive bids of four of 4 
sult nro made primarily to shut out 
the opponents, They should 
made only on hands containing 4% 
long suit and little or no defon- 


They indicate to partner that 
with the suit you namo aa trump 
you will take cight tricksa—in other 
words, you are ovor-bidding your 
hond two tricks. If partner happens 
to hold two tricks, you may be auc- 
ceasful in making your contract. 

Under the writer's system, it is 
impossible to make a pre-emptive 
bid of three and they rarcly, if ever, 
accomplish their purpose. If you 
ure going to pre-empt, do 80 high 
enough to keep the opponents from 
arriving at their best declaration, 

There are a few handa where pre- 
emptive bids of four ino minor 
wil work out well, but generally 
the best minor pre-emptive bid is 
an original bid of five. You are 
only endeavourinie to accomplish 
one thing with a pre-emptive bid, 
and that is to stop the opponents 
from bidding. 


. Support Pre-Emptive Bids 


As partner has told you that he 
in overbidding his hand two tricks 
and haa no side atrength, do ‘not 
support his pre-emptive bid even 
though you hold quick tricks, un- 
less you hold a sufficient number of 
quick tricks to asaure a slam. ‘The 
matn point. to. remember in pre- 
emptive bids is that if the op- 
ponents should overeall partner's 
‘original suit bid of four and your 
hand contains only one to two 
quick tricks, you can muke a good 
nucrifice bid by supporting partner. 
With. partner advertising no defen- 
sive strength and you with very 
little, undoubtedly the vpponents 
have game. 

When you support partner's bid. 
holding one trick, you can expect 
to go down only two tricks, and in 
the majority of cases for a very 
good sacrifice. If you hold two 
tricks, partner should be down only 
one. 

Pre-emptive bids can also be 
made after the bidding Is opened; 
e.g. partner bids one heart, oppo- 
ments pass, and you bid three or 
four spades. In cither case, this 
svould be purely a pre-emptive bid 
informing partner that your hand 
was wofthless for anything but 
spndea. There are very few hands, 
however, where this will prove suc- 
ceasful as generally, the only one 
it shuts out from bidding {s part- 
ner. 

Again, supposing that opponents 
opened with one heart. bid 
three or four spades, this would be 
two or three more than necessary, 
and would jn no way be a forcing 
bid. Partner would immediately 
take it for a pre-emptive bid in 
which you were announcing. that 
you had nothing but spades, This 


| type of bid {s used more frequently, 


than the overenl] of partner's sult 
with a pre-empt.

PLAYGROUNDS FOR 

+> COLONY 

_’ ROTARY CLUB DECISION 
NOT’ UNANIMOUS: ‘ 


i WLK. PRE-HISTORY 


The question of providing more 
children’s . playgrounds in the 
Colony came up for discussion at 


be | yesterday's Rotary Club lunchcon, 


which was held in Messrs, “Lanc 
Crawford’s restaurant under the 
chairmanship of Sir William Hor- 
nell, It was unanimously decide 
to maintain the two existing 
grounds, 
viding two more met ‘with some 
dissent, 

Tho Chairman welcomed tho 
following visltors.—The Rev. E. C. 
H. Tribbeck, Capt. Hopkins, of the 
as. Yatshing, Rotarian G. J. Me- 
Carthy and Rotarlan Yinson Lee, 
both of Shanghal. 

The Chairman said: On August 
25, the Club carried the following 
resolution unanimously; “That ap- 
plication be made to ‘the Govern- 
ment for the use of Blake Gardens 
and an aren opposite the Soldiers’ 
and Snilora’ Home on the Praya 
East Reclamation; that the Govern- 
ment be asked to enclose and level 
these grounds, and that tho Rotary 
Club equip them ‘with games op- 
paratua and pay the salary of 0 
Play Director by a levy on each 


member of the Club, and that the. 


Chinese Y.M.C.A. be Invited to 
avcuro the Director and supervise 
his work.’” ; ‘ 

You will notice that the Rotary, 
Club's undertaking to equip the 
playgrounds with games apparatus 


and pay the salary of Play Diree- | 


tor was not limited to any specific 
period. However, on writing pri- 
vately, as I did; on August 26 Inst, 
to the Hon. Colonial Secretary, I 
said that the © Rotary Club's. 
guarantee to find the cost of the 
inittal apparatus and the solaries 
of a Play Director and grounds- 
men was for a year, 
no doubt that if the experiment was 
successful, and I was sure it would 
be successful, that the Rotary 
Club would assume practically 
permanent responsibility for this 
work, which it would almost cer- 
tainly try to extend. . 


Extension Recommended. 


You have before you, in The 
Bidletin, certain. proposals of the 
Community Service Committee. 
The first recommendation ia that 
the present work be maintained. 
The third recommendation is that 
the present arrangements with the 
Chinese Y.M.C.A.’ be continued, 
The second recommendation is that 
two new grounds be applied for, and 
fourthly that the amount required 
be raised by voluntary subscriptions 
from members of this Club. 

Now, I hold. that-the Club haa 
committed itself to running, as 
organised playgrounds, the exist- 
ing ground at Wanchai and that 
which occuples half of Blake Gar- 
dens. I propose, however,.‘to put 
it to the Club whether or not the 
existing plnyground work should 
be maintained and maintained on 
the same basis, that is to aay at a 
cost as per the estimate in The 
Brulctin of about $1,860, and also 
whether or not the present arrange- 
ment with the Chinese Y.M.C.A. 
should be continued. . 

There ts also before the,Club s 
proposal that the playground work 
should not only’ be maintained but 
extended. I sincerely hope there 
is no one here wlio would wish to 
close down. the work which the Club 
has'‘already taken up, Thero may 
be members who feel tho existing 
work should be maintained but not 
extended, Both those who ‘feel 
that the work should be maintained, 
and those who feel that the work 
should be extended, should vote 
for the resolution which I shall 
shortly put to you, that the play- 
ground. work of the Rotary Club 
be maintained. . 


Additional Cost. 


Having got this resolution ac- 
cepted, if it is accepted, I shall put

THE HONG KONG SOCIETY 
FOR THE PRTECTION OF 
CHILDREN. 


' 


What to do to help a child. 
Anyone knowing of a child who 
has been assaulted, neglected or 
ill-treated in a manner likely’ to 
cause unnecessary suffering or 
injury to health, or knowing of 
a parent who fs seeking advice on 
any matter concerning 2 child, 
would be doing an act of kindness 
by communicating at once with— 
Tho Hon. Secretarica, HKS.P.C. 
c/o G.P.O. Box No. 613, Hongkong 
or St. George's Building, Top floor. 
All further atepa will be taken, 
and expenses borne, by the Society. 
The Informant’s name will be 
kept strictly private, except in 
cases where malice In proved,

THE HONGKONG. TELEGRAPH. WEDNESDAY, MAY 11, 1932. 


MORE TROUBLE 
LIKELY 


JAPAN MOVING IN 
MANCHURIA 


Shanghai, May 10. 
Apprehension — that Shanhal- 
kuan might, at any moment, be- 
come the contre of another Sino- 
Japancso “ineldent,” has -been 
created by reports olleging that 
Japanese military authoritics 


have demanded the rightgto aup- | 


press and arrest Chinese ‘Volun- 
teers” within an arca of 27 miles 
wost of the Great Wall. ° 
General Ho Chu-kuo, Chineso 
Commander-in-Chief of the 
-Shanhaikuan aren, has refused 
to comply, and now he Is await- 
ing: the Japancse military © au- 
thorities’ next step. : 
It ig reported that the Japanese 
forcos aro strengthening their 
defences in the vicinity of 
Shanhaikuan and that under 
their protection, 100 Manchukuo 
police have occupied the Shan- 
haikuan railway station. 
The situation fs described 
tense.—Reuter’s Special. 


Peking Reports. 


Peking, May 10. 
Vernacular newspapers state 
that a “certain high official” went 
to Nanking by air a few days ago, 
and, it is suggested in some 
cles, he intends to discuss 
Shanhaikuan situation with 
Central Government. 
Japanese military authorities 
nre continually accusing the Chi- 
nese authoritics at Shanhal- 
kuan, of allowing large numbers 
of voluntcors and munitions to 
enter Manchuria, and are report- 
ed to he threatening drastic 
action.—Reuter. : 


War Office Explains. 


Tokyo, May 10. 
— Announcing the arrival of the 
main body of the Fourteenth 
Division at Dairen from Shang 
hai, the War Office explains that 
the transfer of reinforcements to 
Manchurian was necessitated by 
the inadequacy of the Manchukuo 
police military to cope with the 
increasing activities of bandits 
and {insurgents who, with the 
growth of the spring foliage, are 
able to conceal thelr movements 
and carry out their depredations 
over n greatly extended arca. ~ 
‘The War Office states that 
there are now ‘six divisions in 
Manchurian and Korea.—Reuter. 


_ Harbin Excitement. 


Harbin, May 10. 

A state of high tension exists 
here, and residents have been 
thrown into a state of apprehen- 
sion. Hostilities betweon Chin-

| BE SILVER-PROUD. 


Just a few moments given to the 
care of table silver and cutlery 
every day will halve the time spent 
on the aveckly cleaning. ~ 

Never put the silver and‘knives 
away carelessly, or they will scrat- 
ch each other. There is an ex- 
cellent silver tidy which is simply 
a board with.a row of pegs. Be- 
tween each -is a gap into which 
apoons and forks are fitted, tha 
whole being slipped into a drawer 
in sideboard or dresser, and used 
aya tray when laying the tablo. | 
« Knives should be kept in a rack 
which ia fitted with slots for ench 
blade. This can be put‘in the cut- 
Jory drawer. and as it holds two 
dozen knives it fs n quick way of 
keeping a check on tho numbers.

THE WATER SITU ATION 


“Although ‘there Is ne ocension 
for alarm, the Colony’s waiter 
supply has su decreased as 4 ton- 
sequence of the lack of rain that 
the authorities have deemed it 
wise to impose fresh restrictions 
‘ay from to-day. Tt has been found 
that the recent culling down of 
the hours of supply, 8° far from 
resulting in less water being cqn- 
sumed, hag, had a precisely oppa- 
site effect, This is due te hoard- 
jng—the drawing off of supplies 
for use when nonc ia coming 
through the mains, much of which 
is Inter. thrown away when .the 
service is resumed, Past experi- 
ence his shown that this is the 
kind of thing which might be 
expected, however regrettable it 
may be. The fact js that people 
have not yet been brought to a 
conactousness of the disservice 
which they do to themselves and 
the community at large by need- 
lesa wastage _of water. It is 
enough for most peoplé that water 
periodically comes through the 
mains, and if it so happens that 
they have put some aside, then 
they make no ‘bones about dis- 
carding the surplus when fresh 
eupplics come through. : 

How this problem is to be coun- 
tered, it is dificult to see. Moro- 
over, there [sp no guarantee. that 
the aamo practice... Will not be 
followed under the now restric- 
tions now being imposed. It would 
bo impracticable to mako a bye- 
jiew prohibiting hoarding of 


the Latin-American republics and 
Japan, many people have expected 
an preneral improvement . in the 
{rade intercourse of the countries 
concerned. — These expectations 
were based on the conditions which 
developed in Germiiny at the time 
of the immense post-war period 
of inilation. Observers, however, 
tend to overlook one vital differ- 
ence between the position then and 
now, When Germany found her- 
self in the * throes of inflation, 
there was & universal demand for 
commodities, due to the partial 
stoppage of world trade during 
the war, At present the world 
finds itself faced with an enor-

BURTON HOLMES 
HERE - 


TO MAKE HONGKONG 
TRAVELOGUE 


Burton Holmes, the eminent 
traveller, lecturer and motion pie- 
ture photographer, who recently 
celebrated his fortieth season on 
the lecture platform, arrived in 
Hongkong on the Empresas of Asia 
to-days 

As he wanders ground the 
world, to lands and far ports on 
the seven seas, he heads a merry 
parade, for wherever Burton 
Holmes goes, there follows in his 
wake o motley throng, who view 
his travelogue films in the picture 
theatres of the world. ; 

Burton Holme’s travelogues are 
famous, Tor seven years, during 
the silent era, he waa associated 
with Paramount, and his weekly 
travel pictures were shown 
wherever Paramount films were 
sereened, le omuakes — talking 
travel films for the M.G.M. people 
nowadays, and very fine films. they 
ure, foo, ag can be told by those 
who saw his reeent film of the 
Yangtse Basin, svreened in Hong. 
kong a few weeks ago, 

Mr. Holmes has travelled ¢x- 
tonsively, and has visited Hong. | 
kong on six previous — aceasions, 
livery now and then he feéls the 
urge to-fira places.” yo now le ig 
undertaking, joyously, another and 
more comprehensive tour around 
the world. : 

This morning ino an interview 
with a representative of — the 
Teleqraph he yvecalled that his first 
visit here occurred just after 
Admiral Dewey = defented — the 
Spaninrds in the | Amerlean- 
Spanish war of 1898. On, that 
occasion Mr. Holmes met and 
photographed the famoua Ameri- 
can Admiral in Hongkong. 

“I've photographed Hongkong 
from several angles," he sald, 
“and it has always proved one of 
the principal features in my films 
showing how the rest of the world 
lives and what it does, This time 
I'm going to catch no lot of 1932 
Tfongkong, and will incorporate 
it In a talking travelogue of:my 
round-the-workt tour, Most of 
my previous films have been short 
travelogues, but this will be a 
longer effort. , . 

“Seven Inanguages are used in 
sound recording. I speak English, 
French, Spanish and Italian my- 
sclf, and expert linguists. trana- 
Into’ into Portuguese, Swedish 
and Germgn. z 

“As a matter of fact I can 
speak German algo, ‘but we don’t 
get enough beer in America to get 
the accent right!” : : 

Mr. Holmes, who is accompanied 
by Andre ln Varre, the eminent | 
camern-man, who ts his partner, 
will proceed from Hongkong to 
tho Dutch Enat Indies, via Manila. 
From there he will return home 
vfn Ceylon, the Suez, Egypt, 
Palestine and the Continent.

WRIT FOR BREACH OF 
PROMISE. 


The Central News “announces 
that a writ for breach of promise 
to marry has been served on Primo 
Carnera, the Italian boxer, on be- 
half of Miss Emelia Tersini, a 
waitress in a Soho restaurant. 


Miss Tersini, who is a dark-eyed 
wir! of 21, is the niece of Mr 
KMttore Nani, the proprietor of a 
Soho restaurant. She lives with 
her mother in Meard-street, S to. 


News for the Mother.- 

Mrs. Tersini,. mother of Miss 
Emilia. Tersini, was unable either 
lo deny or confirm the report that 
a writ hid been served. 2 

“This ts entirely news to me,” 
said Mrs, Tersini. “If my daugh- 
ter ja taking such action she has 
certainly not mentioned it to me. 
This Is the first I have heard of it.”

FRENCH WARSHIP 
ON LAST VOYAGE. 


——— 


THE WALDECK ROUSSEAU 
IN HONGKONG 


The French armoured cruiser 
Waldeck Rousseau has arrived hore 
from Shanghai. The flagship of 
Vice-Admiral Herr, Commander-in- 
Chief of the French Naval Forces 
in Far Eastern waters, she Js pra- 
ceeding back to France, via Saigon, 
on being decommissioned, the Vico- 
Admiral being rolieved by Admiral 
Rerthelot, the new Commander-in- 
Chief, who 1s expected here towards 
the end of the month on the cruiser 
Primauguct. 

This is tho last occasion’on which 
Hongkong will be sccing tho 
Waldeck Rousseau, which, with her 
six funnels, has stood out con- 
spicuously from nmongst the many 
naval visitors to this port. In 
point of fact, the Waldeck Rousseau 
isa very old type of vessel, one of 
the heavily armoured type, which in 
pre-war days took a middie place 
between the dreadnought and the 
ordinary crulser. : 

Rullt in 1908, 
__ Built in 1908, she has fourteen 
Z6-inch guns, eight 3-Inch guns and 
two anti-nireraft) «guns. Her 
accondary armament consists of 
10 9-pounders, 2 %-pounders and 
1 1-pounder. She js heavily 
armoured, 1 6%-inch belt protect- 
ing her vitals, while the turrets, 
turret bnaes and casemates are cor- 
respondingly well protected, the 
conning tower being specially rejn- 
foreed by a ten-inch plate. no 

The cruiser displaces slightly 
over 12,000 Lons, as compared with 
the 10,000 ton cings of cruiser nu- 
thoriaed by. the Washington Treaty 
and of which there are at present 
five included in the . British 
aquadron on the China Station. 
~ The Waldeck Rousseau was to 
have been scrapped some few years 
nyro, but for rensons of economy the 
French Minister of Marine decided 
to put her again into commission 
for n new lease of life on the Far 
Enstern Service. She was recon- 
ditioncd and a few improvements 
added such as the instarment 0 
two antl-nireraft guns and the ad- 
dition of two xeaplancs to her 
equipment, Since her arrival here 
xho has revved na the flagship of 
two Admirals, Vice-Admiral Herr 
being the last to fly his pennant 
from aboard her. It Is announced 

‘that her career will terminate on 
her return to France, when she will 
be broken up. 

; New Flagship. 

The new flagship of Vice-Admiral 
Herr’s successor, the Primauguet, 
is not altogether a stranger to 
these parts, as she first came out 
here in 1926 on a clreumnavigating. 
voyage, during which she passe 
through Hongkong. Whilst hero 
an interesting demonstration, and 
one which’ then was a novel sight 
for Hongkong was given, when an 
neroplane, with which she Was 
equipped, was Inunched by catapult, 

‘the machine afterwards making a 
sustained flight over the harbour. 

One of a class of three sister 
vessely; the  Primauguet, in- 
anugurates a new speedy type of 
criufser, which has ‘since been 
emulated by other continental 
navies. Of armour she has 
practically none worth speaking 
about, but with her speed “of 34 
knots and her 8 6.1 inch guna, she 
in considered very effective as O 
scouting vessel. She displaces 
7,249 tons. Each of. her class of 
yversels carries two scouting type 
seuplane, launched £8 “Indicated 
above, by catapult situated abaft 
the afler turret. Depth charges 
for anti-submarine operations are 
also carried. ; 

With fer inclusion the French 
Naval Forces in Far Eastern waters 
is being brought up to date. Re- 
jcently a fleet of submarines arrived 

Ifrom France at their base in Sai- 

Lyon, while at the same time a num- 
ber of new gunboats have been pro- 
jected, and are being built also for 
service in these waters.

“\. GUNBOATS SENT TO 


‘ WEST RIVER 


Canton, May 10.. 

‘Troop movements on the West 
River, in particular in Shihing 
and Takhing, have been inter- 
preted in some quartors a8 Sa 
tlonary measures taken by tho Gov- 
ernment to cnsure that the recal- 
eftrant gunboats will not surrender. 
to the Kwangsl Government. It 
-{a believed that the gunboat com- 
manders have sent representa- 
tives to Interview tho Kwangasi 
Military Commanders In Nanning. 

A few gunboats loyal to Gen. Chan 
Chai-tong have been dispatched to 
the West River where they will 
maintain watch ovor the move- 
monta of other gunbonts under 
Admiral Chan Chak, whose re- 
signation and refusal to accopt 
the Government post of high ad- 
vigor to the new naval department 
havo ralsed tho possibility of 
union between the navy and Gen- 
eral Chang Wei-cheung, for reafs- 
tance to the Government measures 
for tho reorganisation of their 
dopartments. 


HKwangsi Leaders’ Role. 


Many believe that the next de- 
velopment in the South-western 
situation may come from. Kwangal 
‘and in this connexion General 
Chan Chai-tong has sent Mr. Feng 
Chu-wan, the recently resigned 
Finance Commissioner, to Nan- 
ning to interview Generals Li 
Chung-yen and Pei Chung-hal. 
Tho appointment of Mr. Feng 
Chu-wan to an important military 
post hore has been hinted at,in 
semi-official circles und this has 
been regarded as an effort to re-

STRUCK BY LIGHTNING. 


ALARMING EXPERIENCE OF 
AIR LINER 


London, May 10. 

Further detalles regarding the 
Imporial Airways 88-seater - alr 
linor Horatius, which was struck 
by lightning yesterday when 2,000 
fect up over Kont on its way to 
Paris, showed that the pilot, 
Captain Jones, one of the Airway’s 
most experienced airmen, was un- 
perturbed. by the unusual ex- 
porience, The vistbility was nil, 
and the machine was proceeding 
through heavy sleet storm, when'a 
blinding flash occurred which dis- 
abled the radio equipment, com- 
plotoly blew out tho cockpit win- 
dows and slightly damaged two of 
the propellers, : 

The pilot scribbled ao note 
telling the passengers that overy- 
thing was ull right, and that he 
was flying to.Croydon. 

This | he did, despite thick 
wenther and a useless compass, 
making a perfect landing. Thir- 
teen passengers Including several 
women had remained quite calm, 
and _at once transferred to another 
air-liner in which Captain Jones 
flew them to Paris. No one was 
hurt and the’ machine was damaged 
but repairable.—British Wireless.

“The Night Angel.” 


The King’s Theatre offers for the 
public delectation one of the most ab- 
sorbing and exciting tnikies of the 
current season—"The Night Angel,” 
with Nancy Carroll and Fredric March 
in the leading starring roles. , 

In addition t» the fact that the 
piquant Miss Carroll and the hand- 
some Mr. March are in the picture 
there js the interest which accrues to 
the name of Edmund Goulding, the 
directorial genius who wrote and di- 
rected this romantic drama of modern 
days and nights In Prague, the modern 
capital of Czecholslovakia, 

‘Goulding was the author and direc- 
tor of Glorin Swanson’s well-remem- 
béred ploy “The Trespasser,” and of 
the Nancy Carroll-Phillips Holmes 
record-break—"The Devil's Holiday.” 

In “The Night Angel” Goulding has 
choxen an entirely new locale, a fresh 
viewpoint in talkie stories. 7 

“The Night Angel” is. Nancy's third 
picture to be made in Paramount's 

ew York studio. The others were 
“Laughter” with March, and “Stolen 

Heaven" with Phillips Holmes. 

March was recently mado o star by 
Paramount as the result of his re- 
markably splendid work in “The 
Royal Family of Broadway,” ‘Man- 
slaughter,” “Laughter,” “Honour 
Among Lovers," and others, * 

A competent cast of well-known 
Brondwoy performers supports the 


stars. 
“The Painted Desert” 


Bill Boyd returned to his old stamp- 
ing ground when he went to the desert 
outside of Flagstaff, Arizona, for 
location scenean of "The Painted De- 
sert,” his new Pathe atar picture 
which will be on view at the Central 
Theatre to-morrow, 

Before Boyd took up motion pictures 
as a carcer be was more or less of a 
soldier of fortune and for several 
years tried his luck in and around 
Fiogstaf! where he swung ao double 
edged axo in the lumber camps. 

After considerable experience as ao 
woodchopper Bill acquired a dance hall 
at Lake Mar, a resort cight miles 
from Flagstaff, This was one of the 
first places he visited when he reached 
the Arizona town. It was here that 
the star held a powwow reunion with 
several dozen of hia cronies of the 
days when a chuck wagon looked lke 
the Ritz to him, F

Parrel] Macdonald, Clarke Gable, Will 
Walling, Charles Sellon, James Don- 
lan, Wnde Boteler, Richard Cramer,

ho docs the cavoman stuff, or claé 
ho docs the cynical stuff. : 


Both methods are wrong. 
ono with sense can dee that. 


Tho caveman technique — ig 
clumsy, while tho cynic is rather 
insulting. Women loathe being 
taken for granted. Treated, for- 
sooth, like the brondminded maa- 
culine creatures they like to 
appear! ‘ 


Now if I were a.man I should 
treat the modern young woman in 
just the way that I happen to 
know she would like to be treated, 
I'd revive tho love-makling art.. ] 
shouldn't dream of descending to 
caveman methods and crumpling 
her favourite dress while  pro- 
testing the heat of my passion. 
Nor should I care to offend the 
poor thing by ao cynical take-me- 
or-lenve-me-what do I cart sort of 
attitude. 


My technique would be far in 
advance. I would make her feal 
that for her sweet sake I would 
not only shed my blood In great 
romantic drops, cross deserts, 
climb mountains, ford rivers, etc., 
but go into politics, wear a top 
hat, get a divorce, or even cat 
prunes. My Wrie would be this. 
t would show’her by every action 
that she, and she alone, was the 
queen of my soul. Shyness? 
Modesty? My attitude would 
that of a knight. How could one 
so unworthy expect to be loved by 
one so perfect? How could he 
dare to hope that the air he 


Any- 


‘| dreithed should also be breathed 


by a woman so wonderful. . .. 

Now that js the way these things 
should be done. It’s not the cave- 
men we want. And it's not the 
cynics. They haven't the faintest 
conception of (Iinttering a woman, 
of mnking her fee] she has only 
to lift one finger and a great big 
man will either explode or burst 
into tears. 

If I were a man I should know 
how to treat the modern young 
woman. I should know how to 
bring out the feminine side, not 
the masculine side. I -should 
know how to make her fee) charm- 
ing and dainty, a kind of exqul- 
site vase that might easily get 
broken, and yet to give her that 
glorious sense of feminine power, 
rather dangerous and _ thrilling. 
For modern young women are just 
the same as their grandmothers 
really, in spite of the clothes they 
wear, and the Innguage they use, 
and the nonsense they talk. : 

Give them romance, and you'll 
see that I'm right. Make love to 
them properly for once. Give 
them a jolly good dose of the good 
old pedestal business, 


TREASURY BONDS. 


ENCOURAGING RESPONSE TO]. 


SALE BY TENDER 


wet London, May 10. 
The response to the three per 
cent. Treasury Bonda, offered for 
sale by tender last week, proved 
much greater than was expected. 
Applications amounted to £120,- 
140,000 and tenders were accept- 
ed to the extent of £110,000,000, 
which realised an average price 
of £97 168. 1.97d. per cent. or 15. 
197d. above the minimum price. 
The amount = subscribed .s0 
largely exceeds the estimates 
formed by the money market that 
it was suggested the Government 
departments might have taken a 
major, portion of the bond. This 
suguestion has been disposed of 
by the Secretary to the Treasury, 
who, in a reply to a House of 
Commons question stated that the 
total umount subscribed by the 
Government department, or in- 
stitutions acting under the re- 
commendations or instructions of 
the Government, was less than 
£10,000,000.— Reuter.

Hongkong’s Pre-History, 


Owing to the major portion of 


‘the time having been taken up with 


the playground discussion, an ad- 
dress entitled “The Pre-History of 
Hongkong,” by Professor J. L. 
Shellshear, was unavoldrSly cur- 
tailed, and the speaker was not able 
to make more than brief references 
to his subject. ‘ 

He explained that the subject 
dealt with the cultures of early 
man, and explained that 20 polished 
stone axe heads had been found in 
Hongkong. In a period of about 
five years something like 4,000 
specimens had been found in the 
Colony. He referred to interesting 
finds at’ Castle Peak of stone im- 
plements of smal! diameter which, 
he said, were almost exactly similar 
to the oldest cultural implements 
known in Indo-China. ’ 

Professor Shellshear also stated 
that-a bronze . burial ground had 
been found at Aberdeen, where 
bronze axes, apeara and swords had 
been found. 


Philippine Islands. 


After pointing out that 260,000 
specimens had been found in the 
Philippine Islands, and commenting 
on the great work in this connexion 
being dono by the French in Indo- 
China, Professor Shellshear sald 
that interest in the subject Iny in 
finding what connexion there was 
between such places us China and 
Hongkong, the Philippincs, Indo- 
China and Java, and how mankind 
and his culture had spread. 

The speaker handed round a num- 
ber - of pager which were 
examined by those present with 
mreat ee M

OBITUARY. 


FAMOUS SZECHUAN WAR 
LORD AND DICTATOR 


Shanghai, Mny 10. 


According to Chineso reports, 
the famous Szechuan war lord, 
Yang Sen, who was proscribed by 
the Nattonal Government. in 1930 
has dicd as a result of .an in- 
fectious tumour. However, until 
his death, he retained command 
of his troops, ruling part of Sze- 
chuan with all the powers of a 
Dictator. 


The report of his death has not 
been- confirmed from other sources 
—Reutor.

IN PARLIAMENT 


, London, May 10. 

Warm tributes to the momory of 
the late Prosident Doumer were 
paid by both Houses of Parllament 
torday.. In the House of Lordn, an 
address requesting the King to ox- 
press on behalf of the House, 
adhorrence at the crime and 
sympathy with the Government of 
people of France in_ their grief, 
was moved by Lord Hallaham, He 
anid the whole world was shocked 
at the news of the murderous at- 
tack on President Doumer and its 
fatal outcome. 

That a noble life given to public 
service should be atruck down by 
the irresponsible hand of an as- 
saasin was a shock.to tho moral 
sense of clvilscd communitics. 
The late President had left be- 
hind him a memory of personal 
integrity, patriotism and unflinch- 
ing courage which could only 
deepen for the French | people 
their gonse of Joss, but which 
surely would be an inspiration to 
his country in the days to come. 

Lorda Ponsonby and Rending 
supported the motion. . 

In the House of. Commons o 
similar addresa was moved by Mr. 


Stanley Baldwin and seconded by]: 


the Opposition lender, Mr. Lans- 
bury. 

Both motions were unanimous: 
ly approved in silence.-—

EGYPT MAKES AN 
EFFORT 
WIN DOUBLES IN, DAVIS | 
CUP CONTEST. - 


Geneva, May 10, 

Egypt won the third of their 
Davia Cup matches with Italy to- 
day, having lost the first two. In
